The given equation of the parabola is \( y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \).

**Vertex:**  
Comparing with the standard form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), we find \( h = 1 \) and \( k = 5 \). So, the vertex is at \( (1, 5) \).

**Focus:**  
Here, \( a = 1 \). The focus is located at \( (h, k + \frac{1}{4a}) = (1, 5 + \frac{1}{4}) = (1, \frac{21}{4}) \).

**Directrix:**

I need to find the equation of a parabola that passes through three given points: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). 

First, I recall the general form of a quadratic equation:
y = ax² + bx + c

Since all three points lie on the parabola, each point must satisfy the equation. 

I'll substitute each point into the equation to create a system of equations.

For the point (0, 4):
4 = a(0)² + b(0) + c
This simplifies to:
c = 4

Now, I have one constant value: c = 4.

Next, using the point (2, 8):
8 = a(2)² + b(2) + 4
Simplifying:
4a + 2b + 4 = 8
Subtracting 4 from both sides:
4a + 2b = 4

Similarly, for the point (-1, 3):
3 = a(-1)² + b(-1) + 4
Simplifying:
a - b + 4 = 3
Subtracting 4 from both sides:
a - b = -1

Now I have two equations with variables a and b:
1. 4a + 2b = 4
2. a - b = -1

I can solve this system using substitution or elimination. Let's use the elimination method.

First, multiply equation 2 by 2 to align coefficients for b:
2a - 2b = -2

Now add this result to equation 1:
(4a + 2b) + (2a - 2b) = 4 + (-2)
6a = 2
So, a = 2/6 = 1/3

Substitute a back into equation 2 to find b:
1/3 - b = -1
Adding b and subtracting 1 from both sides:
1/3 + 1 = b
4/3 = b

Now I have the values of a, b, and c:
a = 1/3
b = 4/3
c = 4

Finally, substitute these into the general form to get the equation of the parabola:
y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4
</think>

To determine the equation of the parabola that passes through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4), we can use the general form of a quadratic equation:

\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]

**Step 1: Substitute the points into the general form**

For point (0, 4):
\[
4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \\
c = 4
\]

For point (2, 8):
\[
8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \\
8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \\
4a + 2b = 4 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
\]

For point (-1, 3):
\[
3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \\
3 = a - b + 4 \\
a - b = -1 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
\]

**Step 2: Solve the system of equations**

From Equation 2:
\[
a = b - 1
\]

Substitute \( a = b - 1 \) into Equation 1:
\[
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \\
4b - 4 + 2b = 4 \\
6b = 8 \\
b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

Then, find \( a \):
\[
a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 3: Formulate the equation of the parabola**

Substituting back into the general form:
\[
y = \left(\frac{1}{3}\right)x^2 + \left(\frac{4}{3}\right)x + 4
\]

This can be written as:
\[
y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4
\]

**Final Answer:**

{'Final Answer': 'y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4'}

\[
X^2 + 16y = 0
\]

- x₂ = 6, y₂ = -3

k = (1 + (-3))/2 = (-2)/2 = -1

Next, I need to find the radius. The radius is half the distance between the two endpoints, which is the length of the diameter divided by 2. To find the distance between the two points, I'll use the distance formula:

Distance = √[(x₂ - x₁)² + (y₂ - y₁)²]

Calculating each part:
Difference in x-coordinates: 6 - (-2) = 8
Difference in y-coordinates: -3 - 1 = -4

Now, squaring these differences:
(8)² = 64
(-4)² = 16

Adding them up and taking the square root gives the distance (diameter):
√(64 + 16) = √80 = 4√5

So, the diameter is 4√5, which means the radius (r) is half of that: r = (4√5)/2 = 2√5.

With the center at (h, k) = (2, -1) and radius r = 2√5, I can now write the equation of the circle using the standard form:

Simplifying further:

First, I recognize that the general equation of a circle is \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

Since the center of the circle is at the origin \((0, 0)\), I can substitute these values into the general equation:
\[x^2 + y^2 = r^2\]

Next, the circle passes through the point \((0, 5)\). To find \(r^2\), I substitute \(x = 0\) and \(y = 5\) into the equation:
\[0^2 + 5^2 = r^2\]
\[0 + 25 = r^2\]
\[r^2 = 25\]

Finally, to find the radius \(r\), I take the square root of both sides:
\[r = \sqrt{25}\]
\[r = 5\]

Therefore, the equation of the circle is:
\[x^2 + y^2 = 25\]
</think>

To determine the equation of the circle with its center at the origin \((0, 0)\) and passing through the point \((0, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Start with the general equation of a circle:**
   
   The standard form of the equation of a circle is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

2. **Substitute the center coordinates into the equation:**
   
   Given that the center is at the origin \((0, 0)\), substitute \(h = 0\) and \(k = 0\):
   \[
   (x - 0)^2 + (y - 0)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Simplifying this gives:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = r^2
   \]

3. **Use the point \((0, 5)\) to find \(r\):**
   
   Since the circle passes through the point \((0, 5)\), substitute \(x = 0\) and \(y = 5\) into the equation:
   \[
   (0)^2 + (5)^2 = r^2
   \]
   \[
   0 + 25 = r^2
   \]
   \[
   r^2 = 25
   \]

4. **Solve for \(r\):**
   
   Take the square root of both sides to find \(r\):
   \[
   r = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

5. **Write the final equation of the circle:**
   
   Substitute \(r^2 = 25\) back into the simplified equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = 25
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{x^2 + y^2 = 25}
\]

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step.

First, we have the equation:

\[ 9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36 \]

Our goal is to rewrite it in the standard form of an ellipse. The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is:

\[ \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

Where:
- \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

To convert our given equation into this form, we need to divide every term by 36:

\[ \frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36} \]

Simplifying each term gives us:

\[ \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \]

This is now in the standard form of an ellipse. Comparing this with our target equation, we can identify:

- \( a^2 = 4 \) ⇒ \( a = 2 \)
- \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)

So, the semi-minor axis has length 2 along the x-axis, and the semi-major axis has length 3 along the y-axis.

Therefore, the final standard form of the ellipse is:

\[ \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \]

And from this, we can determine that:

- The length of the semi-minor axis (a) is **2**.
- The length of the semi-major axis (b) is **3**.

The center of the ellipse remains at the origin since there are no shifts in the equation.

So, to summarize:
- The standard form of the given equation is \(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).
- The semi-minor axis length (a) is **2**.
- The semi-major axis length (b) is **3**.

These are the key results derived from transforming the original equation into its standard ellipse form.

The given equation represents an ellipse with a major axis along the x-axis. Here's how to find its eccentricity:

1. **Identify Parameters:**
   - \( a^2 = 36 \) ⇒ \( a = 6 \)
   - \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)

2. **Calculate \( c \):**
   - Use the formula \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \):
   - \( c^2 = 36 - 9 = 27 \)
   - \( c = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3} \)

3. **Determine Eccentricity (\( e \)):**
   - Use the formula \( e = \frac{c}{a} \):
   - \( e = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \)

**Final Answer:**  
The eccentricity of the ellipse is \(\boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}}\).

The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is given by:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Given that the ellipse passes through (3, 0) and (0, 2), we can determine \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \).

For the point (3, 0):
- Substitute into the equation: \(\frac{3^2}{a^2} + \frac{0^2}{b^2} = 1\)
- This simplifies to \(\frac{9}{a^2} = 1\) → \( a^2 = 9 \)

For the point (0, 2):
- Substitute into the equation: \(\frac{0^2}{a^2} + \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1\)
- This simplifies to \(\frac{4}{b^2} = 1\) → \( b^2 = 4 \)

Thus, substituting these values back into the standard form equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

Now, to get it into standard form, I need to divide every term by 36 so that the right side equals 1. Let me do that step by step.

(4x²)/36 - (9y²)/36 = 36/36

Simplifying each term, I can reduce the fractions. For the first term, 4 divided by 36 is 1/9, so it becomes (x²)/(9). The second term, 9 divided by 36 is 1/4, so it's (y²)/(4). On the right side, 36 divided by 36 is just 1.

Putting it all together, the equation becomes:

(x²)/9 - (y²)/4 = 1

So now it looks like the standard form of a hyperbola. Comparing this with the general form (x²/a²) - (y²/b²) = 1, I can see that a² is 9 and b² is 4. 

To find 'a' and 'b', I take the square roots. The square root of 9 is 3, so a = 3, and the square root of 4 is 2, so b = 2.

- Vertices at (0, ±5)

To find the standard form of a vertical hyperbola, we use:
\[ \frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

Given \(a = 5\), we have:
\[ \frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

\[ \frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{9} = 1 \]

\[ \boxed{\dfrac{y^2}{25} - \dfrac{x^2}{9} = 1} \]

To find the equation of a hyperbola passing through the point (0, -√10), we start by considering the general form of a hyperbola. 

A vertical hyperbola centered at the origin has the standard form:
\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Given that the hyperbola passes through the point (0, -√10), we substitute this point into the equation:
\[
\frac{(-\sqrt{10})^2}{a^2} - \frac{0^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
Simplifying this, we get:
\[
\frac{10}{a^2} = 1
\]
Thus, \( a^2 = 10 \).

Therefore, the equation of the hyperbola is:
\[
\frac{y^2}{10} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Without additional information such as another point or specific orientation details, we cannot determine unique values for \( b \). Hence, there are infinitely many hyperbolas passing through (0, -√10), each with a different value of \( b \).

### Final Answer
The equation of one such hyperbola is \boxed{\dfrac{y^2}{10} - \dfrac{x^2}{b^2} = 1} where \( b \) is a positive constant.

The standard form of a vertical hyperbola centered at the origin is:

\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Given that the vertices are at (0, ±3), we find \( a = 3 \). The foci are at (0, ±5), so \( c = 5 \). Using the relationship \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \):

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \\
5^2 = 3^2 + b^2 \\
25 = 9 + b^2 \\
b^2 = 16 \\
b = 4
\]

Substituting \( a^2 = 9 \) and \( b^2 = 16 \) into the standard form equation:

\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

The equation of the parabola with vertex at (0, 0) and a focus at (0, 4) is determined by solving for 'a' in the standard form. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**  
   Since the vertex is at (0, 0), the equation simplifies to \( y = ax^2 \).

2. **Determine the Focus Location:**  
   The focus of a parabola in this form is located at (0, 1/(4a)).

3. **Set Up the Equation with Given Focus:**  
   Given that the focus is at (0, 4), set up: \( 4 = \frac{1}{4a} \).

4. **Solve for 'a':**  
   Multiply both sides by 4a to get: \( 16a = 1 \)  
   Therefore, \( a = \frac{1}{16} \).

5. **Write the Final Equation:**  
   Substituting 'a' back into \( y = ax^2 \), we get \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

To determine whether the parabola defined by \( y = 2x^2 + bx + c \) passes through the points \( (3, 4) \) and has its vertex at \( (1.5, 0) \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Use the Vertex Form of a Parabola
The general vertex form of a parabola is:
\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]
where \( (h, k) \) is the vertex.

Given that the vertex is at \( (1.5, 0) \), we substitute these values into the equation:
\[ y = 2(x - 1.5)^2 + 0 \]

### Step 2: Expand the Vertex Form to Standard Form
Expand and simplify the equation:
\[
y = 2(x^2 - 3x + 2.25) \\
y = 2x^2 - 6x + 4.5
\]
So, the standard form of the parabola is \( y = 2x^2 - 6x + 4.5 \).

### Step 3: Verify if the Parabola Passes Through (3, 4)
Substitute \( x = 3 \) into the equation:
\[
y = 2(3)^2 - 6(3) + 4.5 \\
y = 18 - 18 + 4.5 \\
y = 4.5
\]
Since substituting \( x = 3 \) yields \( y = 4.5 \), which does not match the given point \( (3, 4) \), **the parabola does not pass through this point**.

### Final Answer
The parabola defined by \( y = 2x^2 + bx + c \) with vertex at \( (1.5, 0) \) does not pass through the point \( (3, 4) \).

First, I recognize that the given equation Y = 3x² + 6x + 5 is a quadratic equation in standard form.

To find the vertex of this parabola, which gives both x and y coordinates, I'll use the vertex formula. The general form of a quadratic equation is ax² + bx + c, so here a = 3, b = 6, and c = 5.

The x-coordinate of the vertex can be found using -b/(2a), substituting the known values:

x = -6 / (2 * 3) = -1

With the x-coordinate determined as -1, I'll substitute this back into the original equation to find the corresponding y-coordinate:

Y = 3(-1)² + 6*(-1) + 5
= 3(1) - 6 + 5
= 3 - 6 + 5
= 2

Thus, the vertex of the parabola is at (-1, 2), which represents its highest point since the coefficient of x² is positive.

Looking at the given equation, I see that both \( x^2 \) and a linear term in \( x \) are present. This suggests that completing the square would be a good approach to convert it into the standard form.

So, let's start by rearranging the terms:

\( x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12 \)

Now, I'll complete the square for the quadratic in \( x \). To do this, I take the coefficient of \( x \), which is -4, divide it by 2 to get -2, and then square it to get 4. I add this inside the equation on both sides:

\( x^2 - 4x + 4 = -8y - 12 + 4 \)

This simplifies to:

\( (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8 \)

Now, I'll factor out -8 from the right side:

\( (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \)

Comparing this with the standard form \( (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \), it's clear that \( h = 2 \) and \( k = -1 \). The vertex of the parabola is therefore at \( (h, k) = (2, -1) \).

\( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \)

Subtracting 8y and 12 from both sides gives me \( x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12 \). Completing the square correctly by adding 4 to both sides leads to \( (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \), which matches the standard form with vertex at (2, -1).

To convert the given equation into the standard form of a parabola and find its vertex, follow these steps:

**Given Equation:**
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

**Step 1:** Rearrange the terms to group \( x \) terms together.
\[ x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12 \]

**Step 2:** Complete the square for the quadratic in \( x \).
- Take the coefficient of \( x \), which is -4, divide by 2 to get -2, and then square it to get +4.
\[ (x^2 - 4x + 4) = -8y - 12 + 4 \]
\[ (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8 \]

**Step 3:** Factor out the common term on the right side.
\[ (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \]

Now, the equation is in standard form:
\[ (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \]
where \( h = 2 \), \( k = -1 \), and \( 4p = -8 \).

**Step 4:** Identify the vertex.
The vertex of the parabola is at \( (h, k) = (2, -1) \).

Given the directrix \( y = 3 \) and the vertex at \( (0, 0) \), we need to find the equation of the parabola. Since the directrix is above the vertex, the parabola opens downward.

The standard form of a vertical parabola opening downward with vertex at the origin is:
\[ x^2 = -4py \]

Here, \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also from the vertex to the directrix). The directrix is 3 units above the vertex, so:
\[ -p = 3 \implies p = -3 \]

Thus, the equation of the parabola is:
\[ x^2 = 4py \]
Substituting \( p = -3 \):
\[ x^2 = 4(-3)y \implies x^2 = -12y \implies y = -\frac{1}{12}x^2 \]

To find the value of \( y \) when \( x = -2 \):
\[ y = -\frac{1}{12}(-2)^2 = -\frac{1}{12}(4) = -\frac{4}{12} = -\frac{1}{3} \]

Therefore, the value of \( y \) is:
\[ \boxed{-\dfrac{1}{3}} \]

I need to find the equation of the parabola in the form y = ax² + bx + c that passes through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4).

First, I'll substitute each point into the general form to create three equations.

Using (0, 4):
y = a(0)² + b(0) + c ⇒ 4 = 0 + 0 + c ⇒ c = 4

Now, with c known, substitute the other points:

For (-1, 3):
y = a(-1)² + b(-1) + 4 ⇒ 3 = a - b + 4 ⇒ a - b = -1

For (2, 8):
y = a(2)² + b(2) + 4 ⇒ 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 ⇒ 4a + 2b = 4

Now solve the system:
a - b = -1
4a + 2b = 4

Multiply the first equation by 2: 2a - 2b = -2
Add to the second equation: 6a = 2 ⇒ a = 2/6 = 1/3

Substitute a back into a - b = -1:
(1/3) - b = -1 ⇒ -b = -4/3 ⇒ b = 4/3

So, a = 1/3, b = 4/3, c = 4

To find the equation of the parabola that passes through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4), we can use the general form of a quadratic equation:

\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]

**Step 1: Substitute Each Point into the Equation**

For each point \((x, y)\), substitute \(x\) and \(y\) into the equation to create three equations.

- **Point (0, 4):**
  \[
  4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \\
  4 = c
  \]

- **Point (-1, 3):**
  \[
  3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + c \\
  3 = a - b + c
  \]
  
- **Point (2, 8):**
  \[
  8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + c \\
  8 = 4a + 2b + c
  \]

**Step 2: Solve the System of Equations**

From the first equation, we know that \(c = 4\). Substitute this into the other two equations:

- **Equation from Point (-1, 3):**
  \[
  3 = a - b + 4 \\
  a - b = -1
  \]
  
- **Equation from Point (2, 8):**
  \[
  8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \\
  4a + 2b = 4
  \]

**Step 3: Solve for \(a\) and \(b\)**

We have the following system of equations:
\[
\begin{cases}
a - b = -1 \\
4a + 2b = 4
\end{cases}
\]

Multiply the first equation by 2 to make it easier to eliminate \(b\):
\[
2a - 2b = -2
\]

Add this result to the second equation:
\[
(4a + 2b) + (2a - 2b) = 4 + (-2) \\
6a = 2 \\
a = \frac{1}{3}
\]

Substitute \(a = \frac{1}{3}\) back into the first equation to solve for \(b\):
\[
\frac{1}{3} - b = -1 \\
-b = -1 - \frac{1}{3} \\
-b = -\frac{4}{3} \\
b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

**Step 4: Write the Final Equation**

Now that we have \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\), substitute them back into the general form:
\[
y = ax^2 + bx + c
\]
\[
y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4
\]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the parabola is:

\[
\boxed{\, y = \dfrac{1}{3}x^{2} + \dfrac{4}{3}x + 4 \,}
\]

To determine the equation of the circle whose endpoints of its diameter are given as \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\), we can follow these steps:

**Step 1: Find the Center of the Circle**

The center \((h, k)\) is the midpoint between the two endpoints. The midpoint formula is:
\[
(h, k) = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Plugging in the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center of the circle is \((2, -1)\).

**Step 2: Find the Radius of the Circle**

The radius \(r\) is half of the diameter. First, we need to find the length of the diameter using the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Plugging in the given points:
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
\]
Therefore, the radius \(r\) is:
\[
r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

**Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle**

The standard equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substituting the values we found:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
\]
Simplifying:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
\]

The given equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) can be converted into the standard form of a circle by completing the square for both variables.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Group Terms:**  
   Start by grouping the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms together.
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

2. **Complete the Square for \( x \):**  
   Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -4 \). Half of that is \( -2 \), and squaring it gives \( (-2)^2 = 4 \).
   
3. **Complete the Square for \( y \):**  
   Take half of the coefficient of \( y \), which is \( +2 \). Half of that is \( +1 \), and squaring it gives \( (1)^2 = 1 \).

4. **Add Squares to Both Sides:**  
   Add these squares to both sides of the equation to maintain balance.
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y + 1) = 11 + 4 + 1
   \]

5. **Simplify and Factor:**  
   Simplify each group and factor them into perfect squares.
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
   \]

This is the standard form of a circle with center at \( (2, -1) \) and radius \( 4 \).

The intersection points between the line \( y = x + 3 \) and the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \) can be found by solving the equations simultaneously.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Substitute \( y = x + 3 \) into \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \):**
   \[
   x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
   \]

2. **Expand the equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   2x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
   \]

3. **Subtract 17 from both sides to set the equation to zero:**
   \[
   2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
   \]
   Simplify by dividing all terms by 2:
   \[
   x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
   \]

4. **Factor the quadratic equation:**
   \[
   (x + 4)(x - 1) = 0
   \]
   So, \( x = -4 \) or \( x = 1 \).

5. **Find corresponding \( y \) values using \( y = x + 3 \):**
   - For \( x = -4 \):
     \[
     y = (-4) + 3 = -1
     \]
   - For \( x = 1 \):
     \[
     y = 1 + 3 = 4
     \]

6. **Verify the points in both original equations:**
   - **Point (-4, -1):**
     - \( y = x + 3 \) → \( -1 = -4 + 3 \) ✔️
     - \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \) → \( (-4)^2 + (-1)^2 = 16 + 1 = 17 \) ✔️
   - **Point (1, 4):**
     - \( y = x + 3 \) → \( 4 = 1 + 3 \) ✔️
     - \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \) → \( 1^2 + 4^2 = 1 + 16 = 17 \) ✔️

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\left\{ 
  \begin{array}{l}
    (-4, -1) \\
    (1, 4)
  \end{array}
\right.}
\]

To find the equation of the circle passing through the points \((-2, 8)\), \(P\left(\frac{3}{7}, \frac{2}{7}\right)\), and \(Q(-6, -5)\), we start by assuming a general quadratic form:

\[ x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0 \]

Substituting each point into this equation yields the following system of equations:

1. For point \(P\left(\frac{3}{7}, \frac{2}{7}\right)\):
   \[
   \left(\frac{3}{7}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{2}{7}\right)^2 + D\left(\frac{3}{7}\right) + E\left(\frac{2}{7}\right) + F = 0
   \]
   
2. For point \(Q(-6, -5)\):
   \[
   (-6)^2 + (-5)^2 + D(-6) + E(-5) + F = 0
   \]

3. For point \((-2, 8)\):
   \[
   (-2)^2 + (8)^2 + D(-2) + E(8) + F = 0
   \]

Solving this system of equations gives \(D = -\frac{37}{7}\), \(E = \frac{10}{7}\), and \(F = \frac{13}{7}\). Substituting these back into the general form, we get:

\[ x^2 + y^2 - \frac{37}{7}x + \frac{10}{7}y + \frac{13}{7} = 0 \]

Multiplying through by 7 to eliminate fractions:

\[ 7x^2 + 7y^2 - 37x + 10y + 13 = 0 \]

To express this in standard form, complete the square for both \(x\) and \(y\):

For \(x\):
- Coefficient of \(x\) is \(-\frac{37}{7}\). Half of it is \(-\frac{37}{14}\), and its square is \(\left(\frac{37}{14}\right)^2 = \frac{1369}{196}\).

For \(y\):
- Coefficient of \(y\) is \(\frac{10}{7}\). Half of it is \(\frac{5}{7}\), and its square is \(\left(\frac{5}{7}\right)^2 = \frac{25}{49} = \frac{100}{196}\).

Adding these squares to both sides:

\[ (x - \frac{37}{14})^2 + \left(y + \frac{5}{7}\right)^2 = \frac{1105}{196} \]

Thus, the standard form of the circle's equation is:

\[
(x - \frac{37}{14})^2 + \left(y + \frac{5}{7}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{\sqrt{1105}}{14}\right)^2
\]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the circle is:

\[ 7x^2 + 7y^2 - 37x + 10y + 13 = 0 \]  
or in standard form,  
\[ (x - \frac{37}{14})^2 + \left(y + \frac{5}{7}\right)^2 = \frac{1105}{196} \]

**Center:** \(\left( \frac{37}{14}, -\frac{5}{7} \right)\)  
**Radius:** \(\frac{\sqrt{1105}}{14}\)

Given the problem statement, we need to find the equation of a right circular cylinder with specific dimensions. The key steps are as follows:

1. **Understanding the Problem**: We are given that the cylinder has vertical length 12 units and horizontal length 8 units. These lengths likely refer to the height and diameter of the cylinder.
2. **Diameter and Radius**: The horizontal length of 8 units refers to the diameter of the circular base. Therefore, the radius is half of this diameter, which is \( r = \frac{8}{2} = 4 \) units.
3. **Center of the Cylinder**: The cylinder is centered at the point (3, -2).
4. **Equation of the Cylinder**: Using the standard form of the equation of a circle in the x-y plane, we shift the center to (3, -2). The equation becomes:
   \[
   (x - 3)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Substituting the radius \( r = 4 \), we get:
   \[
   (x - 3)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 16
   \]

Thus, the equation of the cylinder is \boxed{(x - 3)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 16}.

First, I examine the given equation of the ellipse: x²/36 + y²/9 = 1.

Next, to find the eccentricity (e), I use the formula e = √(1 - (b²/a²)).

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Identify the Given Equation:**
   The equation provided is:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]
   
2. **Recognize the Standard Form of an Ellipse:**
   The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin with major axis along the x-axis is:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   where \( a > b \).

3. **Determine \( a \) and \( b \):**
   Comparing with the standard form:
   - \( a^2 = 36 \) ⇒ \( a = 6 \)
   - \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)

4. **Calculate Eccentricity (\( e \)):**
   The formula for eccentricity is:
   \[
   e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}}
   \]
   
5. **Substitute the Values:**
   \[
   e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{9}{36}} = \sqrt{1 - 0.25} = \sqrt{0.75}
   \]

6. **Simplify the Result:**
   \[
   e = \sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \approx 0.866
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{e \approx 0.866}

   - Each focus is at (±40, 0), so \( c = 40 \).

4. **Calculate the semi-minor axis length 'b' using the relationship \( a^2 = b^2 + c^2 \):**
   - Substitute known values: \( 50^2 = b^2 + 40^2 \)
   - \( 2500 = b^2 + 1600 \)
   - Solve for \( b^2 \): \( b^2 = 2500 - 1600 = 900 \)

     \]
   - Substitute \( a^2 = 2500 \) and \( b^2 = 900 \):
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{900} = 1

The given equation \( \frac{X^2}{4} + \frac{Y^2}{9} = 1 \) represents an ellipse. The standard form of an ellipse is \( \frac{(X - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(Y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where (h, k) is the center, a is the semi-major axis length, and b is the semi-minor axis length.

For this ellipse:
- \( a^2 = 4 \) so \( a = 2 \)
- \( b^2 = 9 \) so \( b = 3 \)

The area of an ellipse is given by the formula \( \pi ab \).

Calculating the area:
\( \pi * 2 * 3 = 6\pi \)

{'Final Answer': '6π'}

To determine the foci and vertices of the given ellipse \(\frac{(X - 1)^2}{9} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{16} = 1\):

- **Center**: The center is at (1, -2).
  
- **Vertices**: Since \(a^2 = 16\) (\(a = 4\)), the vertices are along the major axis, which is vertical. Thus:
  - Top vertex: \((1, -2 + 4) = (1, 2)\)
  - Bottom vertex: \((1, -2 - 4) = (1, -6)\)

- **Foci**: The distance from the center to each focus (\(c\)) is calculated by \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\). Here, \(b^2 = 9\) (\(b = 3\)), so:
  \[
  c^2 = 16 - 9 = 7 \implies c = \sqrt{7}
  \]
  Therefore, the foci are at:
  - Upper focus: \((1, -2 + \sqrt{7})\)
  - Lower focus: \((1, -2 - \sqrt{7})\)

**Final Answer**:  
The vertices are at (1, 2) and (1, -6), while the foci are at \(\left(1, -2 + \sqrt{7}\right)\) and \(\left(1, -2 - \sqrt{7}\right)\).

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

- \( a = 4 \) (distance from center to each vertex)
- \( c = 6 \) (distance from center to each focus)

\[

\]

Substituting the known values:

\[
6^2 = 4^2 + b^2 \\
36 = 16 + b^2 \\
b^2 = 36 - 16 \\
b^2 = 20
\]

\[
\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{20} = 1

The given equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) can be converted to the standard form of a hyperbola.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Divide both sides by 36** to normalize the right-hand side:
   \[
   \frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
   \]
   
2. **Simplify each term:**
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
   \]

The equation is now in the standard form of a hyperbola.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\left\{\text{Final Answer}\right\} : \left\{\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\right\}}
\]

\]
where \( a \) is the distance from the center to each vertex along the y-axis.

\[

\]

\[

\]

\[
\frac{3^2}{10} - \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1 \\
\frac{9}{10} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1
\]

**Step 4: Solve for \( b^2 \)**

\[

-\frac{4}{b^2} = \frac{1}{10}
\]

\frac{4}{b^2} = -\frac{1}{10}
\]

Given the problem of finding the equation of a parabola with two points and knowing that one of them is the vertex, we start by using the vertex form of a parabola's equation. The general form for a parabola with vertex \((h, k)\) is \(y = a(x - h)^2 + k\). 

For this problem, the vertex is given as \((5, -2)\), so we substitute these values into the vertex form:

\[ y = a(x - 5)^2 - 2 \]

To determine the coefficient \(a\), we need another point on the parabola. Suppose this other point is \((x_1, y_1)\). We substitute \(x_1\) and \(y_1\) into the equation:

\[ y_1 = a(x_1 - 5)^2 - 2 \]

Solving for \(a\):

\[ a = \frac{y_1 + 2}{(x_1 - 5)^2} \]

Without another point, we cannot determine a unique value for \(a\). Therefore, the equation of the parabola remains in the form:

\[ y = a(x - 5)^2 - 2 \]

where \(a\) can be determined if another point is provided.

### Final Answer
The equation of the parabola cannot be uniquely determined with only the given vertex. To find it, you need at least one more point. If provided with such a point, the equation can be found using the method described above. The final answer would then be in the form \boxed{y = a(x - 5)^2 - 2} where \(a\) is determined by an additional point.

6x = -6
x = -1

3 - 6 = -3
-3 + 5 = 2

Therefore, the y-coordinate of the vertex is 2. 

Putting it all together, the vertex of the parabola defined by Y = 3x² + 6x + 5 is at (-1, 2). This point represents the minimum value of the function since the parabola opens upwards.

x = -6 / (2*3) = -6/6 = -1

This confirms our earlier calculation that the x-coordinate of the vertex is -1. Substituting back gives us y = 2 as before.

To find the vertex of the given parabolic equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \), we'll convert it into its vertex form.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

2. **Rearrange terms to group \( x \) and constants on one side:**
   \[ x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12 \]

3. **Complete the square for the quadratic in \( x \):**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -4 \). Half of that is \( -2 \).
   - Square this value: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \).

4. **Add this square to both sides of the equation:**
   \[ x^2 - 4x + 4 = -8y - 12 + 4 \]
   Simplify:
   \[ (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8 \]

5. **Factor out the common term on the right side:**
   \[ (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \]

6. **Express in vertex form \( (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \):**
   Comparing with the standard vertex form, we have:
   \[ h = 2 \]
   \[ k = -1 \]
   
7. **Identify the vertex:**
   The vertex of the parabola is at \( (h, k) = (2, -1) \).

To determine if there exists a non-degenerate parabola with a vertex at \((0, 3)\) and a directrix \(y = 3\), we start by recalling the definition of a parabola. A parabola is the set of all points equidistant from a fixed point called the focus and a fixed line called the directrix.

Given the vertex \((0, 3)\) and the directrix \(y = 3\), we note that the vertex lies on the directrix. For any point on the parabola, the distance to the focus must equal the distance to the directrix. However, if the vertex is already on the directrix, the only way this condition can be satisfied for all points is if the focus coincides with the vertex.

Thus, the focus would also be at \((0, 3)\). The equation of the parabola then simplifies to:

\[
\sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |y - 3|
\]

Squaring both sides, we get:

\[
x^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (y - 3)^2
\]

This simplifies to:

\[
x^2 = 0
\]

Which implies \(x = 0\). This represents a vertical line at \(x = 0\) with the vertex at \((0, 3)\), which is a degenerate conic section.

### Final Answer
The equation represents a degenerate conic section, specifically the vertical line \boxed{x=0}.

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a parabola that passes through three points: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). Hmm, where do I start? Well, I remember that the general form of a quadratic equation is y = ax² + bx + c. Since it's a parabola, this should work.

2. a - b = -1

(2a + b) + (a - b) = 2 + (-1)

The quadratic equation of the parabola passing through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4) is found by solving a system of equations derived from these points in the general form \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \). 

1. Using the point (0,4):  
   \( 4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \Rightarrow c = 4 \).

2. Substituting (2,8) into \( y = ax^2 + bx + 4 \):  
   \( 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \Rightarrow 4a + 2b = 4 \Rightarrow 2a + b = 2 \).  

3. Substituting (-1,3) into the equation:  
   \( 3 = a(1) - b + 4 \Rightarrow a - b = -1 \).

Solving the system of equations:
- From step 2 and 3:  
  Adding \( 2a + b = 2 \) and \( a - b = -1 \):  
  \( 3a = 1 \Rightarrow a = \frac{1}{3} \).  

- Substituting \( a \) into \( a - b = -1 \):  
  \( \frac{1}{3} - b = -1 \Rightarrow b = \frac{4}{3} \).

Thus, the equation is:
\[ y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \].

Final Answer: The equation of the parabola is \( y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4 \).

To solve the given problem:

**Equation:** X² = -16y  
This is a quadratic equation representing a parabola.

**Step 1: Identify the Vertex Form**
The standard form of a vertical parabola is (X - h)² = 4p(y - k), where:
- (h, k) is the vertex.
- p determines the direction and distance from the vertex to the focus.

Comparing with X² = -16y:
- h = 0, k = 0 ⇒ Vertex at (0, 0).

**Step 2: Determine the Value of 'p'**
From the equation:
4p = -16  
So, p = -16 / 4 = -4.

**Step 3: Locate the Focus**
Since p is negative (-4), the parabola opens downward. The focus is located at (h, k + p) which is (0, 0 - 4) ⇒ (0, -4).

**Conclusion:**
The vertex is at (0, 0) and the focus is at (0, -4).

The user presented a system of equations involving a circle and a line. The system is as follows:

\[
\begin{cases}
x^2 + y^2 = 1 \\
x + y = 0
\end{cases}
\]

To solve this system, we substitute \( y = -x \) from the second equation into the first equation:

\[
x^2 + (-x)^2 = 1
\]
\[
x^2 + x^2 = 1
\]
\[
2x^2 = 1
\]
\[
x^2 = \frac{1}{2}
\]
\[
x = \pm \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}
\]

Thus, the corresponding \( y \) values are:

- When \( x = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \), \( y = -\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \)
- When \( x = -\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \), \( y = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \)

Therefore, the solutions to the system are:

\[
\left( \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}, -\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \right) \quad \text{and} \quad \left( -\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}, \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \right)
\]

### Final Answer
The solutions to the system are \boxed{\left( \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}, -\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \right)} and \boxed{\left( -\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}, \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} \right)}.

The equation of the circle can be found using the given endpoints of the diameter (-2, 1) and (6, -3). 

1. **Find the midpoint** to determine the center:
   \[
   h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2,\quad k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
   \]
   
2. **Calculate the radius** by finding half the distance between the two points (diameter):
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
   \]
   The radius is half of this, so:
   \[
   r = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
   \]

3. **Formulate the equation** using the center (h, k) and radius r:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Substituting the values:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
   \]

**Final Answer**: The equation of the circle is \((x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20\).

Sure! Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Problem:**  
Convert the given equation to the standard form of a circle:
\[ x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \]

**Solution:**

The general form of a circle is:
\[ x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0 \]

To convert it to the standard form, we complete the square for both \(x\) and \(y\) terms.

1. **Rearrange the equation:**
   \[ x^2 - 4x + y^2 + 2y = 11 \]

2. **Complete the square for \(x\):**
   - Coefficient of \(x\) is \(-4\).
   - Take half of \(-4\), which is \(-2\), and square it: \((-2)^2 = 4\).
   - Add and subtract \(4\) on the left side:
     \[ x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4 + y^2 + 2y = 11 \]

3. **Complete the square for \(y\):**
   - Coefficient of \(y\) is \(2\).
   - Take half of \(2\), which is \(1\), and square it: \(1^2 = 1\).
   - Add and subtract \(1\) on the left side:
     \[ x^2 - 4x + 4 + y^2 + 2y + 1 - 1 = 11 \]

4. **Combine like terms:**
   \[ (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 - 5 = 11 \]
   
5. **Move the constant term to the right side:**
   \[ (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16 \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation in standard form is:
\[ \boxed{\left\{ \text{Final Answer} : (x-2)^2 + (y+1)^2 = 16 \right\}} \]

Firstly, I recall that the general equation of a circle is given by:
$$(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2$$
where $(h, k)$ represents the coordinates of the center and $r$ is the radius. 

In this case, the center is at (3, 4), so substituting these values into the equation gives:
$$(x - 3)^2 + (y - 4)^2 = r^2$$

Now, to find out if the circle touches or intersects the x-axis, I need to consider the y-coordinate of the center and how it relates to the radius. The x-axis is located at $y = 0$, so the distance from the center to the x-axis is simply the absolute value of the y-coordinate.

Here, since the y-coordinate of the center is 4, the shortest distance from the center to the x-axis is 4 units. This means that for the circle to touch or intersect the x-axis, the radius must be at least equal to this distance.

Therefore, if $r = 4$, the circle will just touch the x-axis (be tangent to it). If $r > 4$, the circle will intersect the x-axis at two points. However, without knowing the exact value of the radius, we can't definitively say whether the circle touches or intersects the axis.

So, in summary:
- **Radius equals 4:** The circle is tangent to the x-axis.
- **Radius greater than 4:** The circle intersects the x-axis at two points.
- **Radius less than 4:** The circle doesn't touch or intersect the x-axis.

Since there's no information about the radius, we can only provide these conditions based on the given center coordinates.

The problem involves finding the eccentricity of an ellipse given by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form of the Ellipse Equation:**
   The standard form of an ellipse is:
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   where (h, k) is the center, and a > b indicates a horizontal major axis.

2. **Determine \( a \) and \( b \):**
   From the given equation:
   \[
   a^2 = 36 \Rightarrow a = 6 \\
   b^2 = 9 \Rightarrow b = 3
   \]
   
3. **Calculate Eccentricity:**
   The formula for eccentricity (e) of an ellipse is:
   \[
   e = \frac{c}{a}
   \]
   where \( c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2} \).

4. **Compute \( c \):**
   \[
   c = \sqrt{36 - 9} = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3}
   \]

5. **Find Eccentricity:**
   \[
   e = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \approx 0.866
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The eccentricity of the ellipse is \( \boxed{\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}} \).

The equation of the line passing through (3,0) and (0,2) is determined by finding its slope and using the point-slope form.

First, calculate the slope \( m \):
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{2 - 0}{0 - 3} = -\frac{2}{3}
\]

Using the slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \) and substituting one of the points to find \( b \):
\[
0 = -\frac{2}{3}(3) + b \\
b = 2
\]

Thus, the equation of the line is:
\[
y = -\frac{2}{3}x + 2
\]

First, I need to understand what the user is asking for. They want me to find the eccentricity of a hyperbola given by the equation \( \frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1 \).

To do this, I'll recall that the standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin is \( \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where it opens upwards and downwards.

From the given equation, I can identify:
- \( a^2 = 25 \) so \( a = 5 \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) so \( b = 4 \)

The formula for eccentricity (e) is \( e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \).

Plugging in the values:
\( e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{16}{25}} = \sqrt{\frac{41}{25}} = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5} \)

So, the eccentricity is \( \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5} \).

To find the **eccentricity** of the given hyperbola:

\[
\frac{y^2}{25} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

### Step 1: Identify the Standard Form of a Hyperbola
The standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin and opening upwards and downwards is:

\[
\frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

### Step 2: Determine \( a \) and \( b \)
From the given equation:
- \( a^2 = 25 \) ⇒ \( a = 5 \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) ⇒ \( b = 4 \)

### Step 3: Calculate the Eccentricity
The eccentricity (\( e \)) of a hyperbola is given by:

\[
e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}
\]

Plugging in the values:

\[
e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{16}{25}} = \sqrt{\frac{41}{25}} = \frac{\sqrt{41}}{5}
\]

### Final Answer

{'Final Answer': '√41⁄5'}

To find the equation of a hyperbola with a horizontal transverse axis (આડી ટ્રાંસવર્સ અક્ષ) and asymptotes with slopes ±2, we start with the standard form of such a hyperbola:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

The slopes of the asymptotes for this hyperbola are given by \( \pm \frac{b}{a} \). According to the problem, these slopes are ±2. Therefore:

\[
\frac{b}{a} = 2 \implies b = 2a
\]

Substituting back into the standard form equation, we get:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{(2a)^2} = 1 \implies \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{4a^2} = 1
\]

Without additional information (such as the vertices or specific points on the hyperbola), we cannot determine the exact numerical values for \( a \) and \( b \). However, if we assume that the hyperbola is centered at the origin and opens horizontally with asymptotes \( y = \pm 2x \), then any multiple of this equation will satisfy the given conditions.

The hyperbola has foci at (0, ±5) and vertices at (0, ±3), so it opens vertically. The standard form is:

\(\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1\)

Where:
- \(a = 3\) (distance from center to vertex)
- \(c = 5\) (distance from center to focus)
  
Using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\):

\(25 = 9 + b^2 \Rightarrow b^2 = 16\)

So, the equation is:

\(\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1\)
